.

We see from Table
3
that the proposed weak approximation is comparable to the LévyGAN model but significantly more accurate than Davie’s approximation in the fourth moment, Gaussian MMD and EUCFD metrics. However, unlike LévyGAN, the proposed weak approximation (
42
) matches several moments of Lévy area exactly (Theorem
9
) and therefore may be more appealing for the weak simulation of SDEs.
Moreover, since the LévyGAN model is about twice as expensive to evaluate as the weak approximation, it would have been reasonable to use a “two-step” approximation,

\displaystyle\overset{\,\approx}{A}_{0,1}:=\widetilde{A}_{0,\hskip 0.7113pt\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\big(W_{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes W_{\frac{1}{2},1}-W_{\frac{1}{2},1}\otimes W_{1}\big)+\widetilde{A}_{\frac{1}{2},1}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(83)

which, by Brownian scaling, we would expect to have
\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\approx 70\%
of the error as
\widetilde{A}_{0,1}\hskip 0.7113pt
.

Although the proposed weak approximation and LévyGAN are similar in accuracy, due to the difference in evaluation speed, we believe the former performs slightly better (at least when
d\leq 4
). In any case, it is clear that both the proposed moment-matching approximation (
42
) and the machine learning based approach
Jelinčič
et al. (2025)
are significantly more efficient than the traditional Fourier series approximation of Brownian Lévy area.

5.2.2
Heston stochastic volatility model

Following the experiments summarised in section
5.2.1
, the authors of
Jelinčič
et al. (2025)
used the moment-based and LévyGAN approximations to simulate the (log-)Heston model
Heston (1993)
.
The Heston model is a popular stochastic volatility model in mathematical finance due to the semi-analytic formula it gives for European call options
Crisostomo (2015)
. It is given by

\displaystyle dS_{t}

\displaystyle=rS_{t}\hskip 0.7113ptdt+\sqrt{V_{t}}\hskip 0.7113ptS_{t}\hskip 0.7113ptdW_{t}^{1},

(84)

\displaystyle dV_{t}

\displaystyle=\kappa(\theta-\nu_{t})\hskip 0.7113ptdt+\sigma\sqrt{V_{t}}\hskip 0.7113ptdW_{t}^{2},

where
r\in\mathbb{R}
is the discount rate and
\kappa,\theta,\sigma>0
satisfy the Feller condition
2\kappa\theta\geq\sigma^{2}
. In the experiment, we will compute the value of a call option with strike price
K>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T})\big]\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where}\hskip 14.22636pt\varphi(S):=e^{-rT}(S-K)^{+},

(85)

using Multilevel Monte Carlo (MLMC) simulation, introduced by Giles in
Giles (2008)
, for variance reduction. The key idea of MLMC is to write
\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T})\big]
as a telescoping sum,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T}^{L})\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt0})\big]+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell})-\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1})\big],

(86)

where
S^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell}
denotes a numerical solution of the SDE whose accuracy increases with
\ell
.
Typically,
S^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell}
will be a discretization obtained using a step size proportional to
2^{-\ell}
.
The advantage of MLMC is that, when estimating
\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell})-\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1})\big]
at each “level”, one can simulate the numerical solutions
(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell}\hskip 0.7113pt,S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1})
using the same underlying noise. This reduces the variance of the Monte Carlo estimator for
\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell})-\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1})\big]
and thus results in an MLMC estimator that requires fewer finely discretized sample paths.
We refer the reader to
Giles (2015)
for a detailed account of MLMC’s theory and applications.

In the experiment, we shall estimate the call option (
85
) using MLMC with different SDE solvers – including a high order method that will use Lévy area approximations.
Since the Heston model produces an efficient semi-analytic formula for computing call option prices, it is possible to directly estimate weak errors for each numerical scheme.
We summarise the methods below, but would refer the reader to
Jelinčič
et al. (2025)
for full details.

•

Multilevel Monte Carlo with the no-area Milstein method. Due to the slow
O(\sqrt{h}\hskip 0.7113pt)
strong convergence of the scheme, we would only expect the variance of the
\ell
-th level Monte Carlo estimator for
\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell})-\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1})\big]
to be
O(h)
, where
h\propto 2^{-\ell}
.

•

Antithetic Multilevel Monte Carlo with the no-area Milstein’s method
Giles and Szpruch (2014)
and Strang splitting. By coupling
S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1}
with
S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell}
and an antithetic version of
S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell}
, we expect the variance of the
\ell
-th level estimator for
\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell})-\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1})\big]
to be
O(h^{2})
.

•

Multilevel Monte Carlo with the “Strang-Log-ODE” method introduced in
Foster
et al. (2024)
. When used with true Brownian Lévy area, we expect this method will achieve first order strong convergence (thus giving
O(h^{2})
MLMC variance reduction) and second order weak convergence. However, we will instead use the approximate Lévy areas:

–

The weak Lévy approximation given by Definition
7
(“Strang-F” in Figure
8
)

–

The deep-learning-based LévyGAN model from
Jelinčič
et al. (2025)
(“Strang-Net” in Figure
8
)

–

Talay’s weak approximation of
A_{s,t}^{ij}
using
\bar{A}_{s,t}\in\{\pm\frac{1}{2}h\}
(“Strang-T” in Figure
8
)

–

No Lévy area, i.e. we replace
A_{s,t}^{ij}
by
\mathbb{E}\big[A_{s,t}^{ij}\hskip 0.7113pt|\hskip 0.7113ptW_{s,t}\big]=0
(“Strang-NA” in Figure
8
)

However, for all these approximations, the MLMC estimator that we use introduces a fixed bias due to the difference in distribution between
\widetilde{A}
and
\overset{\,\approx}{A}
(see equation (
83
)).

Similar to
Al Gerbi et al. (2016)
;
Iguchi et al. (2025)
, we expect that these approximate Lévy areas could be used with antithetic MLMC to achieve both high order weak convergence and variance reduction.
This would likely involve both
S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1}
with
S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell}
having “antithetic twins” obtained by reflecting the sign of their respective Lévy area approximations (i.e. four simulations based on the same Brownian path). However, we leave this as a topic of future work.

In the experiment, we will simulate the Heston model (
84
) using the methods summarised above and estimate the option price (
85
) with the following parameters:

\displaystyle r=0.1,\hskip 8.53581ptK=20,\hskip 8.53581pt\kappa=2,\hskip 8.53581pt\theta=0.1,\hskip 8.53581pt\sigma=0.5,\hskip 8.53581ptS_{0}=20,\hskip 8.53581ptV_{0}=0.4,\hskip 8.53581ptT=1.

The number of samples taken at each level are determined via the approach of
Giles (2008)
.
In Figure
8
, we report the bias of the MLMC estimators (as a function of the level
L
) as well as the variance of the
\ell
-th level Monte Carlo estimator for
\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell})-\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1})\big]
.

Figure 8:
Top left: Bias of MLMC estimator for the call option (
85
) with the no-area Milstein, antithetic no-area Milstein and the Strang-Log-ODE method (using the LévyGAN approximation of Lévy area).
Bottom left: Bias of MLMC estimator with the Strang-Log-ODE method using the approximations of Lévy area summarised above – including the weak approximation (
42
), LévyGAN model and no area.
Right: Variances of the Monte Carlo estimators at each level
\ell
for estimating
\mathbb{E}\big[\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell})-\varphi(S_{T}^{\hskip 0.7113pt\ell-1})\big]
.

In Figure
8
, we see that only the Strang-Log-ODE method (with approximate Lévy areas) can achieve both second order weak convergence and variance reduction.
Though, as previously noted, the MLMC estimator that we use also introduces a fixed bias that will tend to zero as the error in the Lévy area approximation tends to zero.
In the case of Talay’s weak approximation, which simply replaces each
A_{s,t}^{ij}
with a Rademacher random variable, this limits the accuracy of the MLMC estimator to
2^{-7}
.
However, this is not the case for the proposed weak approximation of Lévy area (
42
) and LévyGAN model, which introduce a much smaller bias of order
2^{-11}
at each level.
Whilst this additional bias could be a potential problem in applications, it is always possible to reduce this bias by “Chen-combining” Lévy area according to (
83
), see
Jelinčič
et al. (2025)
.

Finally, to conclude this section, we will compare the computational times required for a given accuracy by the Milstein and LévyGAN-based Strang log-ODE methods.

(

, Section 5)
is used to determine the number of levels in the MLMC estimator and the number of samples generated at each level. All of the random variables were generated using PyTorch on a GPU, whereas the numerical methods were implemented using NumPy on a CPU.

In Table
4
, we see that the Strang-Log-ODE method with the LévyGAN model achieves root mean-squared errors smaller than
0.1
significantly faster than Milstein.
Therefore, based on the computational times in Table
2
and the results in Figure
8
, we would also expect the proposed weak approximation (
42
) to outperform Milstein.

As a topic of future work, we would like to incorporate the new weak Lévy area approximation into an antithetic Multilevel Monte Carlo framework similar to
Al Gerbi et al. (2016)
;
Iguchi et al. (2025)
.
This would then have the advantages of second order weak convergence and variance reduction without introducing a fixed bias related to the Lévy area approximation.
With this goal, we could also consider MLMC with the following simulations per level:

Simulation

Approximate Lévy areas

Fine (step size
=\frac{1}{2}h
)

X_{1}\otimes W_{s,u}-W_{s,u}\otimes X_{1}

X_{2}\otimes W_{u,t}-W_{u,t}\otimes X_{2}

Antithetic twin

X_{2}\otimes W_{s,u}-W_{s,u}\otimes X_{2}

X_{1}\otimes W_{u,t}-W_{u,t}\otimes X_{1}

=h
)

\big(\frac{1}{4}\big(W_{s,u}-W_{u,t}\big)+X_{1}\big)\otimes W_{s,t}-W_{s,t}\otimes\big(\frac{1}{4}\big(W_{s,u}-W_{u,t}\big)+X_{1}\big)

Antithetic twin

\big(\frac{1}{4}\big(W_{s,u}-W_{u,t}\big)+X_{2}\big)\otimes W_{s,t}-W_{s,t}\otimes\big(\frac{1}{4}\big(W_{s,u}-W_{u,t}\big)+X_{2}\big)

X_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt,X_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,\frac{1}{16}hI_{d}\big)
are independent and give approximate Lévy areas with the correct mean and covariance matrix.

In the above,
u:=\frac{1}{2}(s+t)
denotes the midpoint of the interval
[s,t]
(which has length
h=t-s
).

5.2.3
SABR stochastic volatility model

In this section, we will consider the SABR
3
3

3

Stochastic
A
lpha-
B
eta-
R
ho
model – which is another popular stochastic volatility model used in mathematical finance
Hagan et al. (2002)
;
Cai et al. (2017)
;
Leitao et al. (2017)
;
Cui et al. (2018)
. It is given by the following SDE,

\displaystyle dS_{t}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\hskip 0.7113pt\sigma_{t}(S_{t})^{\beta}dW_{t}^{1}+\rho\hskip 0.7113pt\sigma_{t}(S_{t})^{\beta}dW_{t}^{2},

(87)

\displaystyle d\sigma_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha\hskip 0.7113pt\sigma_{t}\hskip 0.7113ptdW_{t}^{2},

where
\alpha,\beta,\rho\geq 0
.
For simplicity, the authors of
Foster and
Jelinčič (2024)
set
\alpha=1
and
\beta=\rho=0
, giving

\displaystyle dS_{t}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{t}dW_{t}^{1},

(88)

\displaystyle d\sigma_{t}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{t}\hskip 0.7113ptdW_{t}^{2}.

In
Foster and
Jelinčič (2024)
, the simplified SABR model given by (
88
) is converted into Stratonovich form and then simulated using a variety of numerical methods and step size methodologies.
These are the Euler-Maruyama method and the following Stratonovich SDE solvers:

Definition 16

(Heun’s method)
.

Consider the following Stratonovich SDE on
[0,T]
,

\displaystyle dy_{t}=f(y_{t})\hskip 0.7113ptdt+\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}(y_{t})\circ dW_{t}^{i}\hskip 0.7113pt.

(89)

Then for a given number of steps
N
, we define a numerical solution
\{Y_{n}\}_{0\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptn\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptN}
by

\displaystyle Y_{n+1}:=Y_{n}+\frac{1}{2}\big(f(Y_{n})+f(Z_{n+1})\big)h+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\big(g_{i}(Y_{n})+g_{i}(Z_{n+1})\big)W_{t_{n},\hskip 0.7113ptt_{n+1}}^{i}\hskip 0.7113pt,

where
\,Z_{n+1}:=Y_{n}+f(Y_{n})h+\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}(Y_{n})W_{t_{n},\hskip 0.7113ptt_{n+1}}^{i}\hskip 0.7113pt
,
t_{n}:=nh
,
h:=\frac{T}{N}
and
Y_{0}:=y_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt
.

Definition 17

(Splitting Path Runge-Kutta method (SPaRK))
.

For a number of steps
N\geq 1
, we define a numerical solution
\{Y_{n}\}_{0\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptn\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptN}
for the Stratonovich SDE (
89
) by

\displaystyle Y_{n+1}:=Y_{n}

\displaystyle+\bigg(\frac{3-\sqrt{3}}{6}\big(f(Y_{n})+f(Z_{n+1})\big)+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}f(Y_{n+\frac{1}{2}})\bigg)h+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}(Y_{n+\frac{1}{2}})W_{s,t}^{i}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}(Y_{n})\bigg(\frac{3-\sqrt{3}}{6}W_{s,t}^{i}+H_{s,t}^{i}\bigg)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}(Z_{n+1})\bigg(\frac{3-\sqrt{3}}{6}W_{s,t}^{i}-H_{s,t}^{i}\bigg),

where
t_{n}:=nh
,
h:=\frac{T}{N}
,
Y_{0}:=y_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt
and

\displaystyle Y_{n+\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle:=Y_{n}+\frac{1}{2}f(Y_{n})h+\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}(Y_{n})\bigg(\frac{1}{2}W_{s,t}^{i}+\sqrt{3}H_{s,t}^{i}\bigg),

\displaystyle Z_{n+1}

\displaystyle:=Y_{n}+f(Y_{n+\frac{1}{2}})h+\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}(Y_{n+\frac{1}{2}})W_{s,t}^{i}\hskip 0.7113pt.

Remark 15
.

Taylor expanding the SPaRK method produces the following
O(h)
terms,

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\hskip 0.7113ptj\hskip 0.7113pt=\hskip 0.7113pt1}^{d}g_{j}^{\prime}(Y_{k})g_{i}(Y_{k})\bigg(\bigg(\frac{1}{2}W_{s,t}^{i}+\sqrt{3}H_{s,t}^{i}\bigg)\times\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}W_{s,t}^{j}+W_{s,t}^{i}\times\bigg(\frac{3-\sqrt{3}}{6}W_{s,t}^{j}-H_{s,t}^{j}\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\sum_{i,\hskip 0.7113ptj\hskip 0.7113pt=\hskip 0.7113pt1}^{d}g_{j}^{\prime}(Y_{k})g_{i}(Y_{k})\bigg(\frac{1}{2}W_{s,t}^{i}W_{s,t}^{j}+H_{s,t}^{i}W_{s,t}^{j}-H_{s,t}^{i}W_{s,t}^{j}\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\sum_{i,\hskip 0.7113ptj\hskip 0.7113pt=\hskip 0.7113pt1}^{d}g_{j}^{\prime}(Y_{k})g_{i}(Y_{k})\bigg(\frac{1}{2}W_{s,t}^{i}W_{s,t}^{j}+\mathbb{E}\big[A_{s,t}^{ij}\,|\,W_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,H_{s,t}\big]\bigg),

where the last line follows by the conditional expectation of Lévy area given by (
29
). Hence, the SPaRK method will be asymptotically more accurate than Heun’s method. For details about the improved asymptotic accuracy of SPaRK, see
(
Foster and
Jelinčič, 2024
, Appendix A)
.

In addition, the experiment of
Foster and
Jelinčič (2024)
also investigates different step size strategies. These are constant step sizes (that is,
h=\frac{T}{N}
) and the following two variable step sizes:

Definition 18

(Previsible variable step sizes)
.

Suppose we approximate the solution of (
88
) at time
t_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt
by
(\widetilde{S}_{t_{n}}\hskip 0.7113pt,\widetilde{\sigma}_{t_{n}})
. Then we can define the next step size as
h(\widetilde{\sigma}_{t_{n}})
with

\displaystyle h(\sigma):=\log\big(1+C\sigma^{-2}\big),

(90)

where
C>0
is a user-specified constant. We would expect that decreasing the value of
C
will increase the number of steps (and thus improve the accuracy of the simulation).

Remark 16
.

The variable step size (
90
) is specific to the simplified SABR model (
88
) and based on the local error of Heun’s method, which is computable by Itô’s isometry as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(S_{t+h}-\bigg(S_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{t}\big(1+e^{-\frac{1}{2}h+(W_{t+h}^{2}-W_{t}^{2})}\big)\big(W_{t+h}^{1}-W_{t}^{1}\big)\bigg)\bigg)^{2}\,\bigg]=\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{t}^{2}h(e^{h}-1)\hskip 0.7113pt.

Definition 19

(Non-previsible variable step sizes using an adaptive PI controller
Ilie et al. (2014)
)
.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{n}:=\{W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}\hskip 0.7113pt,H_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}\}
be Brownian increments and space-time Lévy areas. Then we define a modified “Proportional-Integral” (PI) adaptive step size controller, which does not “skip” over times where the Brownian motion was previously sampled:

\displaystyle h_{n+1}

\displaystyle:=\min\big(\{\widetilde{h}_{n+1}\}\hskip-0.35565pt\cup\hskip-0.35565pt\{h>0:t_{n+1}+h\text{ corresponds to a previously rejected time}\}\big),

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{n+1}

\displaystyle:=h_{n}\bigg(\mathrm{Fac}_{\max}\hskip-1.42262pt\wedge\hskip-1.42262pt\bigg(\mathrm{Fac}_{\min}\hskip-1.42262pt\vee\hskip-1.42262pt\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Fac}\cdot C}{e(Y_{n},h_{n},\mathcal{W}_{n})}\bigg)^{K_{I}}\hskip-1.42262pt\bigg(\frac{e(Y_{n-1},h_{n-1},\mathcal{W}_{n-1})}{e(Y_{n},h_{n},\mathcal{W}_{n})}\bigg)^{K_{P}}\,\bigg)\bigg),

where
\{\hskip 0.7113pt\mathrm{Fac}_{\max}\hskip 0.7113pt,\mathrm{Fac}_{\min}\}
are the maximum and minimum factors
h_{k}
can change by,
\mathrm{Fac}
is a safety factor,
\{K_{I},K_{P}\}
are the “integral” and “proportional” coefficients,
C>0
is a user-specified constant and
e(Y_{n},h_{n},\mathcal{W}_{n})
denotes a local error estimator. If
e(Y_{n+1}\hskip 0.7113pt,h_{n+1}\hskip 0.7113pt,\mathcal{W}_{n+1})>1
, then
h_{n+1}
is rejected and
\frac{1}{2}h_{n+1}
will be proposed instead. Otherwise, if the estimator satisfies
e(Y_{n+1}\hskip 0.7113pt,h_{n+1}\hskip 0.7113pt,\mathcal{W}_{n+1})\leq 1
, then
h_{n+1}
is accepted.

In the experiment, we use the values recommended by
Ilie et al. (2014)
,
(K_{I},K_{P})=(0.3,0.1)
,
as well as
\hskip 0.7113pt\mathrm{Fac}_{\max}=10,\mathrm{Fac}_{\min}=0.2
and
\mathrm{Fac}=0.9
(the default values in Diffrax
Kidger ()
).
For the local error estimate
e(Y_{n},h_{n},\mathcal{W}_{n})
, we compare the Heun and SPaRK methods to embedded Runge-Kutta approximations (and refer the reader to
Foster and
Jelinčič (2024)
for the details).

The results of the numerical experiment for these methods are given in Figure
9
and the associated Python code is available at
github.com/andyElking/Adaptive_SABR
.
In addition, Euler, Heun and SPaRK are also implemented in the Diffrax package
Kidger ()
.

Figure 9:
Strong convergence rates estimated for the different approximations of the SABR model (
88
), which was simulated with
S_{0}=0,\sigma_{0}=1
and
T=10
. Due to the counterexample presented in
Gaines and Lyons (1997)
, the authors of
Foster and
Jelinčič (2024)
did not use the Euler-Maruyama method with non-previsible adaptive step sizes. The vector field evaluations used per step are:
1
for Euler-Maruyama,
2
for Heun and
3
for SPaRK.

In Figure
9
, we see that SPaRK gives the best accuracy with constant step sizes, which is expected due to the Lévy area approximation that it uses (see Remark
15
).
In fact, we expect a step of SPaRK to be as accurate as three steps of Heun’s method.

It is also worth noting that the previsible step size is very specific to the simplified SABR model (
88
) and therefore does not immediately extend to more general SDEs. On the other hand, the (modified) adaptive PI step size controller is generic and readily applicable to other problems – especially since we used the default parameter values.
In any case, this experiment shows that methods can achieve an order of magnitude more accuracy when used with adaptive step sizes (both previsible and non-previsible).

In conclusion, the examples in this section (Heston and SABR models) demonstrate that the moments and approximations of Brownian Lévy area detailed in section
3
can be used to improve SDE solvers – whether they are for strong or weak simulation.

5.3
Space-space-time Lévy area examples

In this section, we will present experiments from
Foster et al. (2024)
;
Tubikanec et al. (2022)
;
Foster et al. (2024)
to demonstrate the following (optimal) approximations of space-space-time Lévy area given in section
4
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[L_{s,t}^{ii}\,\big|\,W_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,H_{s,t}\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{5}h\big(H_{s,t}^{i}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{30}\hskip 0.7113pth^{2},

(91)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[L_{s,t}^{ii}\,\big|\,W_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,H_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,n_{s,t}\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{5}h\big(H_{s,t}^{i}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{30}h^{2}-\frac{1}{8\sqrt{6\pi}}\hskip 0.7113ptW_{s,t}^{i}n_{s,t}^{i}h^{\frac{3}{2}}.

(92)

Firstly, in
Foster et al. (2024)
, equation (
91
) was established (with
d=1
) and applied to a scalar SDE.

5.3.1
Inhomogenous geometric Brownian motion (IGBM)

In this section, we will consider different numerical methods for the scalar SDE (
93
), which is often refereed to as “Inhomogenous geometric Brownian motion” (or IGBM).

\displaystyle dy_{t}=a(b-y_{t})\hskip 0.7113ptdt+\sigma y_{t}\,dW_{t}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(93)

where
a\geq 0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
are the mean reversion parameters and
\sigma\geq 0
is the volatility.
Since IGBM has multiplicative noise, it can easily be written in Stratonovich form as

\displaystyle dy_{t}=\widetilde{a}(\widetilde{b}-y_{t})\hskip 0.7113ptdt+\sigma y_{t}\circ dW_{t}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(94)

where
\widetilde{a}:=a+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}
and
\widetilde{b}:=\frac{2ab}{2a+\sigma^{2}}\hskip 0.7113pt
denote the Itô-Stratonovich adjusted parameters.

In the finance literature, IGBM was proposed as a one-factor short rate model
Capriotti et al. (2019)
.
Moreover, as the solution of the SDE (
93
) is both mean-reverting and non-negative, IGBM
is suitable for modelling interest rates, stochastic volatilities and hazard rates.
However, for the purpose of demonstrating the proposed approximation (
91
), we note that IGBM is one of the simplest SDEs that has no known method of exact simulation.
In
Foster et al. (2024)
, the authors investigate the convergence of the following methods for IGBM:

Numerical method

Formula for each step

Log-ODE

\displaystyle Y_{n+1}:=e^{-\widetilde{a}h+\sigma W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}}Y_{n}+abh\big(1-\sigma H_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}\big)\frac{e^{-\widetilde{a}h+\sigma W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}}-1}{-a\widetilde{h}+\sigma W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}}

\displaystyle\left.+\right.ab\sigma^{2}\bigg(\frac{3}{5}hH_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}^{2}+\frac{1}{30}h^{2}\bigg)\frac{e^{-\widetilde{a}h+\sigma W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}}-1}{-a\widetilde{h}+\sigma W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}}\hskip 0.7113pt.

Parabola-ODE

\displaystyle Y_{n+1}:=e^{-\widetilde{a}h+\sigma W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}}\bigg(Y_{n}+ab\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}e^{\widetilde{a}(s-t_{n})-\sigma\wideparen{W}_{t_{n},s}}\hskip 0.7113ptds\bigg).

Linear-ODE

(or Wong-Zakai)

\displaystyle Y_{n+1}:=e^{-\widetilde{a}h+\sigma W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}}Y_{n}+abh\bigg(\frac{e^{-\widetilde{a}h+\sigma W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}}-1}{-a\widetilde{h}+\sigma W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}}\bigg).

Milstein

\displaystyle Y_{n+1}:=\Big(Y_{n}+\widetilde{a}(\widetilde{b}-Y_{n})h+\sigma Y_{n}W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}Y_{n}W_{t_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,t_{n+1}}^{2}\Big)^{+}.

Euler-Maruyama

\displaystyle Y_{n+1}:=\Big(Y_{n}+a(b-Y_{n})h+\sigma Y_{n}W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}\Big)^{+}.

Table 6:
Numerical methods for inhomogenous geometric Brownian motion (IGBM) considered in
Foster et al. (2024)
. Note that the second line of the log-ODE method clearly uses the estimator (
91
) for
L_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt
.

In
Foster et al. (2024)
, IGBM is simulated with the above methods and the following parameters:

\displaystyle a=0.1,\hskip 14.22636ptb=0.04,\hskip 14.22636pt\sigma=0.6,\hskip 14.22636pty_{0}=0.06,\hskip 14.22636ptT=5.

For each numerical method, estimators for the strong and weak convergence rates are computed by Monte Carlo simulation (with 100,000 and 500,000 samples respectively).

Definition 20

(Strong and weak error estimators)
.

Given a number of steps
N\geq 1
, let
Y_{N}

be a numerical solution of (
93
) computed at time
T
using a fixed step size
h=\frac{T}{N}
.
We can define the following estimators for quantifying strong and weak convergence:

\displaystyle S_{N}

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{\hskip 0.7113pt\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(Y_{N}-Y_{T}^{fine}\big)^{2}\Big]}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(95)

\displaystyle E_{N}

\displaystyle:=\Big|\hskip 0.7113pt\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(Y_{N}-b\big)^{+}\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(Y_{T}^{fine}-b\big)^{+}\Big]\Big|\hskip 0.7113pt,

(96)

where the above expectations are approximated by standard Monte-Carlo simulation
and
Y_{T}^{fine}
is the numerical solution of (
94
) obtained at time
T
using the log-ODE method
with a “fine” step size of
\min\left(\frac{h}{10},\frac{T}{1000}\right)
. The fine step size is chosen so that the

L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
error between
Y_{T}^{fine}
and the true solution
y_{T}
is negligible compared to
S_{N}
.
We note that
Y_{N}
and
Y_{T}^{fine}
are both computed using the same Brownian sample paths.

The results of the numerical experiment for simulating IGBM are presented below.
Code for the experiment can be found at
github.com/james-m-foster/igbm-simulation
.

Figure 10:
Strong and weak convergence rates estimated for the different IGBM numerical methods.

In Figure
10
, we see that the log-ODE method with the proposed space-space-time Lévy area estimator (
91
) is the most accurate – for both strong and weak convergence.
However, to confirm this, we will also need take computational cost into consideration.

From Table

, we see that the log-ODE method takes roughly twice as long to generate sample paths as the linear-ODE, Milstein and Euler-Maruyama methods. This is expected as each log-ODE step requires two Gaussian random variables to be generated,
W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}
and
H_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}
, whereas the standard schemes just require
W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}
.

However, after taking computational costs into consideration, it is still clear that the log-ODE method is more efficient than the other numerical methods (see Table
8
).

As IGBM is an ergodic SDE with a known stationary distribution (inverse gamma), it is important to investigate properties of the numerical approximation
Y_{n}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
In
Tubikanec et al. (2022)
, the authors consider the long-term mean and variance of four splitting methods, as well as the log-ODE, linear-ODE, Milstein and Euler-Maruyama methods (Table
6
).

Since
Tubikanec et al. (2022)
conducted very thorough numerical testing, we will only present the results from their final table – which will require us to define the following weak error metrics.

Definition 21
.

Let
\{Y_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
be an approximation of the SDE (
93
) with step size
h
. Then, following
Tubikanec et al. (2022)
, we define the relative conditional mean and variance biases as

\displaystyle\mathrm{rBias}_{h,y_{0}}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{n}\big]\big)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt|\hskip 0.7113ptY_{0}=y_{0}]-\mathbb{E}\big[\hskip 0.7113pty(t_{n})\hskip 0.7113pt|\hskip 0.7113pty_{0}\big]}{\mathbb{E}\big[\hskip 0.7113pty(t_{n})\hskip 0.7113pt|\hskip 0.7113pty_{0}\big]},

\displaystyle\mathrm{rBias}_{h,y_{0}}\big(\mathrm{Var}\big(Y_{n}\big)\big)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathrm{Var}(Y_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt|\hskip 0.7113ptY_{0}=y_{0})-\mathrm{Var}\big(\hskip 0.7113pty(t_{n})\hskip 0.7113pt|\hskip 0.7113pty_{0}\big)}{\mathrm{Var}\big(\hskip 0.7113pty(t_{n})\hskip 0.7113pt|\hskip 0.7113pty_{0}\big)},

and the relative asymptotic mean and variance biases as

\displaystyle\mathrm{rBias}_{h}\big(\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{n}\big]\big)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathbb{E}[Y_{n}]-\mathbb{E}[\hskip 0.7113pty_{\infty}]}{\mathbb{E}[\hskip 0.7113pty_{\infty}]},

\displaystyle\mathrm{rBias}_{h}\big(\mathrm{Var}\big(Y_{n}\big)\big)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathrm{Var}(Y_{n})-\mathrm{Var}(\hskip 0.7113pty_{\infty})}{\mathrm{Var}(\hskip 0.7113pty_{\infty})},

where
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\hskip 0.7113pty_{\infty}]:=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[\hskip 0.7113pty_{t}]
and
\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\hskip 0.7113pty_{\infty}):=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Var}(\hskip 0.7113pty_{t})
, which are known for IGBM.

The experimental results from
(
Tubikanec et al., 2022
, Table 3)
are presented in Tables

.

and

(for Table

estimated for different numerical methods at

. For IGBM, the stationary distribution is inverse Gamma. The densities of

for details.

Therefore, we see that the log-ODE method with the space-space-time Lévy area approximation (
91
) gives the best performance across almost all weak error metrics.
For SDEs similar to IGBM, where computing first and second vector field derivatives is straightforward, we would expect the log-ODE method to be particularly effective.
However, due to the potential difficulties in computing second derivatives, high order splitting methods were developed in
Foster et al. (2024)
– which can use the optimal estimator (
92
).

5.3.2
Noisy anharmonic oscillator

In this section, we shall consider Runge-Kutta methods for the stochastic oscillator,

\displaystyle dy_{t}=f(y_{t})\hskip 0.7113ptdt+\sigma\hskip 0.7113ptdW_{t}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(97)

where
f(x)=\sin(x)
and
\sigma=y_{0}=T=1
. In particular, we will present an experiment

from
Foster et al. (2024)
, which tests the following numerical methods for SDEs with additive noise.

Definition 22

(The “SRA1” Runge-Kutta method for SDEs with additive noise
Rößler (2010)
)
.

For
N\geq 1
, we define a numerical solution
Y=\{Y_{k}\}_{0\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptk\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptN}
of (
97
) by
Y_{0}:=y_{0}
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{k+\frac{3}{4}}

\displaystyle:=Y_{k}+\frac{3}{4}f(Y_{k})h+\frac{3}{4}\sigma\big(W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}+2H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\big)\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle Y_{k+1}

\displaystyle:=Y_{k}+\frac{1}{3}f(Y_{k})h+\frac{2}{3}f\big(\,\widetilde{Y}_{k+\frac{3}{4}}\big)h+\sigma W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\hskip 0.7113pt.

(98)

Definition 23

(Shifted Ralston method
Foster et al. (2024)
based on the approximation (
92
) of
L_{s,t}
)
.

For
N\geq 1
, we define a numerical solution
Y=\{Y_{k}\}_{0\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptk\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptN}
of (
97
) by
Y_{0}:=y_{0}
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{k}

\displaystyle:=Y_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}+\sigma H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma\hskip 0.7113ptC_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{k+\frac{2}{3}}

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{Y}_{k}+\frac{2}{3}\Big(f\big(\,\widetilde{Y}_{k}\big)h+\sigma\hskip 0.7113ptC_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\Big),

\displaystyle Y_{k+1}

\displaystyle:=Y_{k}+\frac{1}{4}f\big(\,\widetilde{Y}_{k}\big)h+\frac{3}{4}f\big(\,\widetilde{Y}_{k+\frac{2}{3}}\big)h+\sigma W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(99)

where each random vector
C_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}
is given by

\displaystyle C_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}

\displaystyle:=\epsilon_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\Big(W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{2}+\frac{12}{5}H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{2}+\frac{4}{5}h-\frac{3}{\sqrt{6\pi}}\hskip 0.7113pth^{\frac{1}{2}}n_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\epsilon_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{sgn}\bigg(W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}-\frac{3}{\sqrt{24\pi}}\hskip 0.7113pth^{\frac{1}{2}}n_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\bigg),

and
n_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}
denotes the space-time swing of
W
over
[t_{k}\hskip 0.7113pt,t_{k+1}]
given by Definition
13
.

Remark 17
.

Taylor expanding the method (
99
) produces the following
O(h^{2})
term:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}f^{\prime\prime}(Y_{k})\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\sigma W_{k}+\sigma H_{k}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma C_{k}\bigg)^{2}h+\frac{3}{8}f^{\prime\prime}(Y_{k})\bigg(\frac{1}{2}W_{k}+H_{k}+\frac{1}{6}\sigma C_{k}\bigg)^{2}h

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\sigma^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(Y_{k})\bigg(\frac{1}{8}W_{k}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}W_{k}H_{k}+\frac{1}{2}H_{k}^{2}+\frac{1}{24}C_{k}^{2}\bigg)h

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\sigma^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(Y_{k})\bigg(\frac{1}{6}hW_{k}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}hW_{k}H_{k}+\underbrace{\frac{3}{5}hH_{k}^{2}+\frac{1}{30}h^{2}-\frac{1}{8\sqrt{6\pi}}\hskip 0.7113pth^{\frac{3}{2}}n_{k}W_{k}}_{=\,\,\mathbb{E}[\hskip 0.7113ptL_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\,|\,W_{k}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113ptH_{k}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113ptn_{k}]}\bigg).

Hence, by Corollary
1
, we see that the proposed method (
99
) is asymptotically optimal.
For additional details regarding the shifted Ralston method, we refer the reader to
Foster et al. (2024)
.

In addition, the authors of
Foster et al. (2024)
also consider the standard Euler-Maruyama method and the following Euler-based method – which has a smaller asymptotic error of
O(h^{2})
.

Definition 24

(Shifted Euler method
Foster et al. (2024)
)
.

We define
\{Y_{k}\}_{0\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptk\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptN}
by
Y_{0}:=y_{0}
and

\displaystyle Y_{k+1}:=Y_{k}+f\bigg(Y_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}+\sigma H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\bigg)h+\sigma W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\hskip 0.7113pt.

(100)

Remark 18
.

Taylor expanding the shifted Euler method (
100
) produces the following:

\displaystyle Y_{k+1}=Y_{k}+f(Y_{k})h+\sigma W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}+\sigma f^{\prime}(Y_{k})\bigg(\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}hW_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}+hH_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}}_{=\,\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}W_{t_{k},t}\,dt}\bigg)+O(h^{2}).

Thus, the shifted Euler method produces an
O(h^{2})
local error whereas Euler-Maruyama has a local error of
O(h^{\frac{3}{2}})
, which is due to the above time integral of Brownian motion.

Since we would expect the two-stage Runge-Kutta methods to achieve
O(h^{1.5})
strong convergence for (
97
), and the Euler-based methods to achieve
O(h)
convergence,
the authors of
Foster et al. (2024)
present the ratio of the strong error estimators (
95
) for different
h
.
Accompanying code can be found at
github.com/james-m-foster/high-order-splitting
.

Figure 11:
S_{N}
estimated for (
97
) with 1,000,000 sample paths, where
N
denotes the number of steps. To illustrate the differences in accuracy between methods, we plot the ratio
S_{N}^{\text{(method 1)}}/S_{N}^{\text{(method 2)}}
.

From Figure
11
, we can see that the shifted Ralston method is almost three times as accurate as the SRA1 scheme – provided that the step size
h
is sufficiently small.
Moreover, as shown in
Foster
et al. (2024)
, the ratio of
0.38
is remarkably close to the theoretical ratio

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}W_{t_{k},t}^{2}\,dt-\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}W_{t_{k},t}^{2}\,dt\hskip 0.7113pt\big|\hskip 0.7113ptW_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\hskip 0.7113pt,H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\hskip 0.7113pt,n_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\Big]\Big)^{2}\,\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}W_{t_{k},t}^{2}\,dt-\frac{3}{16}\big(W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}+2\hskip 0.7113ptH_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}\big)^{2}\Big)^{2}\,\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}},

which equals
\big(\frac{7}{30}-\frac{5}{16\pi}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=0.37
(2.d.p). The terms in the above ratio correspond to

the space-space-time Lévy area approximations used by both Runge-Kutta methods.

Similarly, we see that the shifted Euler method is roughly three times as accurate as the standard Euler-Maruyama method – provided that at least 10 steps are used.
However, as the local errors of the shifted Euler and Euler-Maruyama methods scale differently with
h
(see Remark
18
), it is unclear how to establish a theoretical ratio. Taking computational costs into consideration, we see that the shifted Euler method can clearly outperform Euler-Maruyama for simulating the stochastic oscillator (
97
).

Shifted Ralston

(
99
)

SRA1

Rößler (2010)

Shifted Euler

(
100
)

Euler-Maruyama

Time to simulate 100,000 paths

3.16

2.29

1.91

1.09

with 100 steps per sample path (s)

Estimated time to achieve a

1.79

2.00

23.1

47.0

strong error of
S_{N}=10^{-4}
(s)

Table 12:
Computational times for generating 100,000 sample paths of (
97
) with (a) 100 steps per path and (b) to achieve a given accuracy. All of the numerical methods were implemented in C++.

Due to the computational cost of computing
C_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}
in the shifted Ralston method, we can see in Table
12
that it is only slightly more efficient than the SRA1 scheme.
However, for SDEs where the drift function
f
is much more expensive to evaluate than
C_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}
, we would certainly expect the shifted Ralston method to outperform SRA1.

For general multidimensional SDEs with additive noise, the shifted Ralston method is no longer asymptotically efficient due to the integrals
\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}W_{t_{k},t}^{i}W_{t_{k},t}^{j}\hskip 0.7113ptdt
with
i\neq j
.

Whilst we know how to optimally approximate such integrals (Theorems
14
and
17
),
it is not clear how these estimators translate to derivative-free Runge-Kutta methods.
We will leave the development of such Runge-Kutta methods as a topic of future work.

5.3.3
FitzHugh-Nagumo model

In this section, we shall present our final example, which is a stochastic FitzHugh-Nagumo (FHN) model used to describe the spiking activity of single neurons
Buckwar et al. (2022)
;
León and Samson (2018)
. This FHN model is given by the following SDE with two-dimensional additive noise:

\displaystyle dv_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big(v_{t}-v_{t}^{3}-u_{t}\big)\hskip 0.7113ptdt+\sigma_{1}\hskip 0.7113ptdW_{t}^{1},

(101)

\displaystyle du_{t}

\displaystyle=\big(\gamma v_{t}-u_{t}+\beta\big)\hskip 0.7113ptdt+\sigma_{2}\hskip 0.7113ptdW_{t}^{2},

where
\{v_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
denotes the membrane voltage of the neuron,
\{u_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is an additional recovery variable,
\varepsilon>0
represents the time scale between the
v
and
u
, and the parameters
\beta\geq 0
and
\gamma>0
correspond to the position and duration of an excitation.

The stochastic FHN model is quite challenging to simulate due to the
v^{3}
term, which is not globally Lipschitz continuous on
\mathbb{R}
. However, the function
f(x)=x-x^{3}
does instead satisfy a one-sided Lipschitz condition
(x-y)(f(x)-f(y))\leq(x-y)^{2}
.

Thus, as the drift vector field of the FHN model also has polynomial growth, there are numerical methods in the literature with strong convergence guarantees for (
101
). We will present the FHN experiment in
Foster et al. (2024)
, where two such schemes are considered; the Strang splitting from
Buckwar et al. (2022)
and Tamed Euler-Maruyama method proposed in
Hutzenthaler et al. (2012)
.

To build upon the previous work of
Buckwar et al. (2022)
on Strang splittings, the authors of
Foster et al. (2024)
proposed a high order splitting method – which is based on the optimal estimator (
92
).
For the same class of SDEs with locally Lipschitz drift, we expect that the analysis conducted for Strang splitting in
Buckwar et al. (2022)
should be applicable to this high order splitting.
However, the method (
102
) is quite involved, so we expect this line of research to be more suitable for the simpler “high order Strang splitting” scheme proposed in
Foster et al. (2024)
.
We would refer the reader to
Foster et al. (2024)
for further details regarding these splitting methods.

Definition 25

(High order splitting scheme for the FHN model)
.

For a fixed
N\geq 1
, we define a numerical solution
\{(V_{k}\hskip 0.7113pt,U_{k})\}_{0\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptk\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113ptN}
of (
101
) by
(V_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,U_{0}):=(v_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,u_{0})
and

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}V_{k}^{(1)}\\[3.0pt]
U_{k}^{(1)}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}V_{k}\\[3.0pt]
U_{k}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{1}W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{1}+\sigma_{1}H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{C}_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{1}\\[3.0pt]
\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2}W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{2}+\sigma_{2}H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2}\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{C}_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{2}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}V_{k}^{(2)}\\[3.0pt]
U_{k}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle:=\varphi_{\frac{1}{2}h}^{\text{Strang}}\begin{pmatrix}V_{k}^{(1)}\\[3.0pt]
U_{k}^{(1)}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}\overline{C}_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{1}\\[3.0pt]
\sigma_{2}\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{C}_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{2}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}V_{k+1}\\[3.0pt]
U_{k+1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle:=\varphi_{\frac{1}{2}h}^{\text{Strang}}\begin{pmatrix}V_{k}^{(2)}\\[3.0pt]
U_{k}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{1}W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{1}-\sigma_{1}H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{C}_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{1}\\[3.0pt]
\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2}W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{2}-\sigma_{2}H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{2}\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{C}_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{2}\end{pmatrix},

(102)

where each random vector
\overline{C}_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{\hskip 0.7113pti}
is given by

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{\hskip 0.7113pti}

\displaystyle:=\epsilon_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{i}\bigg(\frac{1}{3}\big(W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{i}\big)^{2}+\frac{4}{5}\big(H_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{i}\big)^{2}+\frac{4}{15}h-\frac{1}{\sqrt{6\pi}}\hskip 0.7113pth^{\frac{1}{2}}n_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{i}W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{i}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\epsilon_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{\hskip 0.7113pti}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{sgn}\bigg(W_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{i}-\frac{3}{\sqrt{24\pi}}\hskip 0.7113pth^{\frac{1}{2}}n_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{i}\bigg),

and
\varphi_{t}^{\text{Strang}}
denotes the Strang splitting proposed by
Buckwar et al. (2022)
for (
101
) when
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=0
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\frac{1}{2}h}^{\text{Strang}}

\displaystyle:=\varphi_{\frac{1}{4}h}^{(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(v-v^{3}),\hskip 0.7113pt\beta)}\circ\varphi_{\frac{1}{2}h}^{\text{linear}}\circ\varphi_{\frac{1}{4}h}^{(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(v-v^{3}),\hskip 0.7113pt\beta)},

where, for vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the ODE solutions
\varphi_{t}^{(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(v-v^{3}),\hskip 0.7113pt\beta)}
and
\varphi_{t}^{\text{linear}}
by

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}^{(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(v-v^{3}),\hskip 0.7113pt\beta)}\begin{pmatrix}v\\[3.0pt]
u\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}v\Big(e^{-\frac{2t}{\varepsilon}}+v^{2}\big(1-e^{-\frac{2t}{\varepsilon}}\big)\Big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\\
u+\beta t\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}^{\text{linear}}\begin{pmatrix}v\\[3.0pt]
u\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle:=\exp\Bigg(t\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\\[3.0pt]
\gamma&-1\end{pmatrix}\Bigg)\begin{pmatrix}v\\[3.0pt]
u\end{pmatrix},

and the explicit formula for the above matrix exponential is given in
(
Buckwar et al., 2022
, Section 6.2)
.

Remark 19
.

Similar to the shifted Ralston method (
99
),
\overline{C}_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}
is designed so that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}h\Big(\frac{1}{2}W_{k}^{i}+H_{k}^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{C}_{k}^{\hskip 0.7113pti}\Big)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}h\Big(\frac{1}{2}W_{k}^{i}+H_{k}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{C}_{k}^{\hskip 0.7113pti}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\frac{1}{8}h\big(W_{k}^{i}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}hW_{k}^{i}H_{k}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}h\big(H_{k}^{i}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{8}h\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{C}_{k}^{\hskip 0.7113pti}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\frac{1}{6}h\big(W_{k}^{i}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}hW_{k}^{i}H_{k}^{i}+\underbrace{\frac{3}{5}h\big(H_{k}^{i}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{30}h^{2}-\frac{1}{8\sqrt{6\pi}}\hskip 0.7113pth^{\frac{3}{2}}n_{k}^{i}W_{k}^{i}}_{=\,\,\mathbb{E}[\hskip 0.7113ptL_{t_{k},t_{k+1}}^{ii}\,|\,W_{k}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113ptH_{k}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113ptn_{k}]}.

Since the
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u\partial v}
derivative of the FHN drift is zero, we do not need to approximate

L_{t_{k}\hskip 0.7113pt,t_{k+1}}^{ij}
for
i\neq j
and therefore, the splitting method (
102
) is asymptotically efficient.

We now present the results of the experiment from
Foster et al. (2024)
, where the FHN model was simulated with the aforementioned numerical methods and the following parameters:

\displaystyle\varepsilon=1,\hskip 14.22636pt\gamma=1,\hskip 14.22636pt\beta=1,\hskip 14.22636pt\sigma_{1}=1,\hskip 14.22636pt\sigma_{2}=1,\hskip 14.22636pt(v_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,u_{0})=(0,0),\hskip 14.22636ptT=5.

Accompanying code can be found at
github.com/james-m-foster/high-order-splitting
.

estimated for the FHN model (
101
) using 1,000 sample paths as a function of
h=\frac{T}{N}
. The estimated strong errors for the Strang splitting and Tamed Euler schemes were taken from
Buckwar et al. (2022)
.

From Figure
12
, we see that the splitting method (
102
) exhibits an
O(h^{\frac{3}{2}})
strong convergence rate and is significantly more accurate than other schemes (for fixed
h
).
For example, the high order splitting method achieves a better accuracy in 320 steps than both the Strang splitting and Tamed Euler approximations do in 10240 steps.
Finally, after taking computational costs into account, we see in Table
13
that the high order splitting achieves a strong error of
10^{-3}
in an order of magnitude less time.

Similar to the previous subsection, the proposed splitting method was designed to use the “diagonal” space-space-time Lévy area estimator,
\mathbb{E}[\hskip 0.7113ptL_{s,t}^{ii}\,|\,W_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113ptH_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113ptn_{s,t}]
.
However, a substantially simpler high order Strang splitting was also proposed in
Foster et al. (2024)
and, after Taylor expanding this splitting, we can obtain the following approximation:

\displaystyle L_{s,t}^{ij}\approx\frac{2-\sqrt{3}}{24}hW_{s,t}^{i}W_{s,t}^{j}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}hH_{s,t}^{i}H_{s,t}^{j}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(103)

which is reasonably close to the estimator
\hskip 0.7113pt\mathbb{E}[\hskip 0.7113ptL_{s,t}^{ij}\,|\,W_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113ptH_{s,t}]=\frac{3}{5}hH_{s,t}^{i}H_{s,t}^{j}+\frac{1}{30}\hskip 0.7113pth^{2}\delta_{ij}
,

but clearly suboptimal.
Thus, we expect that the development of high order numerical methods for high-dimensional additive-noise SDEs will be a topic for future research.

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we have considered the signature of multidimensional Brownian motion with time and presented recent approximations for the non-Gaussian iterated integrals

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}W_{s,u}^{i}\,dW_{u}^{j}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 14.22636pt\int_{s}^{t}W_{s,u}^{i}W_{s,u}^{j}\hskip 0.7113ptdu,

(104)

in terms of the following Gaussian iterated integrals (which can be generated exactly),

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}dW_{u}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{u}dW_{v}\,du,\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{u}dv\,dW_{u}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(105)

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{u}\int_{s}^{v}dW_{r}\,dv\,du\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{u}\int_{s}^{v}dr\,dW_{v}\,du\hskip 0.7113pt,\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{u}\int_{s}^{v}dr\,dv\,dW_{u}\hskip 0.7113pt.

The proposed methodologies for approximating the integrals in (
104
) are heavily based on computing conditional moments with respect to the Gaussian integrals (
105
).
For the well-known “Lévy area” of Brownian motion (corresponding to
\int_{s}^{t}W_{s,u}\otimes\hskip 0.7113ptdW_{u}
), we propose a random matrix that matches several moments of
A_{s,t}
(see Theorem
9
),
but built from standard distributions (normal, uniform, etc) and thus fast to generate.
In
Jelinčič
et al. (2025)
, this approach was shown to empirically outperform the standard Fourier series approximation and was competitive with a recently developed machine learning model.

Since these integrals are all entries in the signature of Brownian motion, they also naturally appear in the Taylor expansions of stochastic differential equations (SDEs).
Therefore, in section
5
, we presented several experiments from the literature where moment-based integral approximations are used to improve accuracy for SDE solvers.

In particular, we observed that approximating the integrals (
104
) by their conditional expectations resulted in higher order methods with state-of-the-art convergence rates.
As a topic of future work, we would like to consider the “adaptive” Langevin dynamics:

\displaystyle dx_{t}

\displaystyle=M^{-1}v_{t}\hskip 0.7113ptdt,

(106)

\displaystyle dv_{t}

\displaystyle=-\hskip 0.7113pt\xi_{t}M^{-1}v_{t}\hskip 0.7113ptdt\hskip 0.7113pt-\nabla f(x_{t})\hskip 0.7113ptdt+\sigma\hskip 0.7113ptdW_{t}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle d\xi_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu}\Big(v_{t}^{\top}M^{-2}\hskip 0.7113ptv_{t}-\frac{1}{\beta}\mathrm{Trace}\big(M^{-1}\big)\Big)dt,

where
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
represent the position and momentum of a particle,
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the
n\times n
mass matrix,
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denotes a scalar potential,
\sigma>0
is a noise parameter,
\beta>0
corresponds to the temperature,
\nu>0
governs the friction
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\{W_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion.
The adaptive Langevin dynamics (
106
) has been studied due to its ergodicity properties and applications to sampling problems
Jones and
Leimkuhler (2011)
;
Leimkuhler
et al. (2020)
;
Leimkuhler and
Shang (2016)
.
If we Taylor expand (
106
), then we only observe the integrals (
104
) in the
\xi
component:

\displaystyle\xi_{t}=\xi_{s}+(\,\cdots)\hskip 0.7113pth+(\,\cdots)\int_{s}^{t}W_{s,u}\,du+\frac{1}{\nu}\sigma^{2}\int_{s}^{t}\Big(\hskip 0.7113ptW_{s,u}^{\top}\,M^{-2}\hskip 0.7113ptW_{s,u}\Big)\hskip 0.7113ptdu+O(h^{\frac{5}{2}}).

Since
\frac{1}{\nu}\sigma^{2}
and
M^{-2}
are constant, we can easily apply our integral estimators to (
106
).
